## W. L. Minckley
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
W. L. Minckley was a prominent ichthyologist and conservation biologist who conducted foundational research on native fish species in the American Southwest, particularly in desert aquatic ecosystems. He was instrumental in advocating for the protection and management of endemic and threatened fish species, serving as a key figure in the Desert Fishes Council and contributing to landmark conservation efforts such as the book "Battle Against Extinction: Native Fish Management in the American West." Minckley's work bridged academic ichthyology with applied conservation biology, establishing him as a leading expert on desert fish ecology and the impacts of habitat alteration on Southwestern aquatic fauna.

## W. M. Smallwood

### Summary
W. M. Smallwood is the author of this 1914 preliminary report on fish diseases in the Adirondacks, published as Technical Publication No. 1 of the New York State College of Forestry at Syracuse University. The report examines the trematode Clinostomum marginatum as the cause of unfit, grub-infested yellow perch and bass in local ponds. He compared infested and nearly parasite-free ponds and studied the parasite's anatomy and early larval stages.

## W.L. Minckley
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
W.L. Minckley was a pioneering ichthyologist and conservation biologist who dedicated his career to the study and preservation of desert fish fauna in the American Southwest, particularly in isolated spring and stream systems of the Sonoran Desert region. He was instrumental in establishing the Desert Fishes Recovery Team and co-editing foundational works such as *Battle Against Extinction*, which synthesized scientific, historical, and ethical perspectives on the decline of these vulnerable species. Minckley's work was central to early conservation efforts for federally endangered desert fishes and helped shape modern approaches to managing relict aquatic populations facing habitat loss and extinction risk.

## Wabash River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Wabash River is a major tributary of the Ohio River in the midwestern United States that serves as an important aquatic system for ichthyological research and fish conservation, supporting diverse lotic and lentic habitats including main-stem river channels, tributary creeks, and associated wetlands. This river system and its tributaries have been extensively studied as sampling locations for documenting fish species distributions, habitat associations, and population dynamics across various substrate types and stream gradients. The Wabash River drainage represents a significant biogeographic region for understanding fish communities and documenting range extensions or new distributional records of freshwater species in the Ohio River basin.

## Wading River

### Summary
Wading River is an 11-mile poor-quality trout stream in Foxboro/Mansfield/Norton, Massachusetts, characterized by sluggish flow, dark brown water, summer temperatures in the 70s-80s, and drainage from numerous warm-water ponds and swamps. This source documents that following 1955 rotenone treatment and fingerling stocking, no trout survival was detected after one year, likely due to high water temperatures at stocking and unsuitable habitat conditions, demonstrating that reclamation cannot overcome fundamental habitat limitations.

## Walker Wildlife Area

### Summary
Walker Wildlife Area is a flooded gravel pit near Grand Junction, Colorado, that is permanently connected to the Colorado River via a 6-meter canal. During the 1974-76 study, this 25.6-hectare site with depths of 0.5-2.0 meters served as one of only two locations where razorback suckers were found in relatively large concentrations in the upper Colorado River Basin, with the species composing 0.8-4.2% of fish catches there. The site had a gravel bottom covered by thick silt layers and cobble-lined shores, and contained both native fish (43% of catch) and introduced species like carp, green sunfish, and black bullhead (nearly one-third of catch), with possible spawning attempts by razorback suckers observed but no evidence of successful reproduction, likely due to predation by introduced species.

### Summary
The walleye (Sander vitreum) is a freshwater percid fish species native to North America that serves as an economically and recreationally important food fish in lakes and rivers throughout the northern United States and Canada. Characterized by their large reflective eyes adapted for low-light vision and predatory behavior, walleyes are sought-after game fish that support significant commercial and recreational fisheries. In conservation and aquaculture contexts, walleye populations are actively managed through hatchery propagation programs and stocking initiatives to maintain viable populations in both natural waters and managed fisheries.

Courtenay Jr. was a prominent ichthyologist and conservation biologist who served as chair of the ASIH (American Society of Ichthyologists and Herpetologists) Environmental Quality Committee and played a significant role in documenting range expansions of fish species, particularly non-native and invasive fish populations in Florida during the 1970s-1990s. ## Warm Springs Pupfish
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Warm Springs Pupfish is a critically endangered endemic species belonging to the Cyprinodontidae family, confined to geothermal spring systems in the Great Basin region, particularly within or near Death Valley National Monument. Like other pupfish species in this region, it represents an extreme example of range restriction and endemism, having evolved in isolation within specific spring complexes and facing severe conservation challenges from habitat degradation, water extraction, and invasive species. These tiny cyprinodont fishes serve as important indicators of spring ecosystem health and are central to regional conservation efforts documented in Death Valley Area Committee planning documents from the 1980s.

## Warm Springs Rapid

### Summary
Warm Springs Rapid is a location in Yampa Canyon at river kilometer 6.56 that marks the downstream boundary of the primary Colorado Squawfish spawning area. The lower 51.2 kilometers of the Yampa River, particularly from Warm Springs Rapid upstream to the vicinity of Harding Hole, serves as critical spawning habitat where fish deposit and fertilize eggs on cobble bars.

## Warmouth
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Warmouth (Lepomis gulosus) is a small freshwater sunfish species native to eastern North America that inhabits sluggish streams, swamps, and backwater areas with dense vegetation. In fisheries and aquatic ecology research, warmouth are studied as components of sunfish communities, particularly in relation to stock management, reproductive biology, and ecological interactions with other centrarchid species like redear sunfish. The species serves as both a research subject for understanding fish population dynamics and fecundity estimation methods, and as a model organism for examining the ecological effects of fish stocking programs in freshwater systems.

## Warmwater Fisheries Project

### Summary
The Warmwater Fisheries Project was a California Department of Fish and Game initiative that by 1955 had transitioned from a purely investigational phase to an active program of controlled introductions of forage fish species. The project aimed to improve warmwater fishing throughout California by introducing non-native forage species like Threadfin Shad, Fathead Minnow, Plains Red Shiner, and Golden Shiner into reservoirs to support predatory gamefish populations.

### Summary
The Water and Power Resources Service (WAPRS) refers to a federal agency or bureau responsible for managing water infrastructure and hydroelectric power generation facilities, which in the context of fish science and conservation involves the stewardship of artificial aquatic environments such as hatcheries and canal systems that may house endangered or listed fish species. As a manager of wholly artificial habitats, WAPRS operations intersect with Endangered Species Act compliance and Section 7 consultation requirements, raising important questions about whether listed species maintained in human-controlled water facilities receive the same regulatory protections as those in natural ecosystems. ## Water Chemistry
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Water chemistry in fish science and aquatic ecology refers to the physical and chemical properties of aquatic environments, including dissolved gases (such as oxygen and carbon dioxide), pH, alkalinity, and mineral composition, all of which directly influence fish physiology, survival, and ecosystem function. These chemical parameters are critical for understanding fish habitat quality, metabolic processes, and the impacts of pollution, algal activity, and industrial effluents on aquatic organisms and their communities. Monitoring and analyzing water chemistry is essential for fish conservation, aquaculture management, and assessing the health and suitability of freshwater and marine ecosystems.

## Water Hyacinth
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Water hyacinth (*Eichhornia crassipes*) is a prolific floating aquatic macrophyte that plays a complex and often contradictory role in fish production systems, simultaneously providing structural habitat for invertebrate prey organisms while potentially competing with fish for nutrients and light in fertilized aquatic environments. In fisheries management and aquaculture contexts, water hyacinth coverage must be carefully controlled, as unmanaged proliferation can reduce the efficiency of sport fish production despite offering favorable microhabitat structure for forage organisms in nutrient-poor conditions. Its effects on fish productivity are strongly dependent on nutrient status, pond management practices, and target species, making it an important variable in pond ecosystem dynamics and an occasional target for biological control using herbivorous fish like grass carp.

## Water Pollution
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Water pollution in aquatic ecosystems refers to the introduction of harmful substances or contaminants into freshwater and marine environments that degrade water quality and negatively impact fish physiology, behavior, and survival. In the context of fish science and conservation, water pollution encompasses various stressors including reduced dissolved oxygen levels, elevated temperatures, and chemical contaminants that compromise fish respiration, metabolism, and overall ecosystem health. Understanding and measuring these pollutants through methods such as polarographic analysis is essential for monitoring aquatic environments and establishing water quality standards necessary for fish conservation and sustainable fisheries management.

### Summary
A Water Pollution Research Laboratory is a specialized facility dedicated to investigating the effects of various contaminants and environmental stressors on aquatic organisms, particularly fish species. These laboratories conduct experimental research to quantify toxicity thresholds, survival rates, and physiological responses of fish to pollutants such as ammonia, salinity changes, and other water quality parameters. By combining analytical chemistry techniques with controlled biological assessments, such facilities provide critical data to inform water quality standards, conservation strategies, and pollution mitigation efforts in aquatic ecosystems.

## Water Quality
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Water quality refers to the physical, chemical, and biological characteristics of aquatic environments that directly influence the health, survival, and reproductive success of fish populations and other aquatic organisms. In the context of fish science and conservation, water quality encompasses parameters such as temperature, dissolved oxygen, pH, nutrient levels, turbidity, and contaminant concentrations, all of which must remain within species-specific tolerance ranges to support viable fish communities. Effective watershed management and monitoring of water quality across jurisdictional boundaries—including public lands, municipal areas, and private holdings—is essential for protecting endangered and threatened fish species and maintaining sustainable fisheries, particularly in regions like the Safford, Arizona watershed where multiple land management agencies must coordinate conservation efforts.

## Water Temperature
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Water temperature is a critical abiotic factor that regulates physiological processes in fish, including metabolic rate, growth, reproduction, and behavior, making it essential for understanding fish ecology and population dynamics. Temperature fluctuations, both seasonal and diel (daily), influence fish distribution, feeding efficiency, and development rates, thereby affecting species survival, community composition, and the overall health of aquatic ecosystems. In fisheries management and conservation, monitoring and understanding water temperature patterns is fundamental for predicting fish productivity, managing habitat quality, and assessing responses to environmental changes such as thermal pollution or climate variation.

## Waters 1961
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Waters 1961 refers to foundational work by Thomas F. Waters establishing quantitative methods for studying fish populations and their ecological relationships in freshwater streams, particularly focusing on energy flow, population dynamics, and the role of fish as indicators of stream ecosystem health. This work became a cornerstone methodology in stream ecology and fisheries science for understanding how fish communities interact with their physical and biological environment. Waters' approach integrated population assessment techniques with ecosystem-level analysis, influencing decades of subsequent research in freshwater conservation and aquatic ecology.

## Watershed Management
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Watershed Management refers to the integrated planning and implementation of practices designed to protect and restore aquatic ecosystems by addressing land-use activities and hydrological processes across entire drainage basins. In the context of fish science and conservation, watershed management encompasses strategies to mitigate anthropogenic disturbances such as logging, water development, and channel modification that degrade fish habitat and stream integrity. This holistic approach recognizes that fish populations and their supporting ecosystems depend on maintaining proper watershed-scale conditions including sediment transport dynamics, riparian vegetation, stream channel morphology, and overall hydrological function.

### Context
> - **Habitat context**: The [[Apache Trout]] inhabits headwater streams likely subject to anthropogenic disturbance from logging activity, situating this species within discussions of [[Watershed Management]] and [[Logging Impacts on Trout Streams]]. - **Trophic ecology**: The denial references ex...

## Watersnake

### Summary
The Common Watersnake (Natrix sipedon) is a non-poisonous aquatic snake found throughout Michigan near any lake, pond, or stream. According to this source, it has mottled brown coloration with dark brown saddle-shaped bands on a gray-brown background, bears 20-25 young alive in late summer, and commonly basks in the sun in trees or bushes 6-8 feet above stream banks. It hibernates on land during cold months.

## Waupaca Chain

### Summary
Waupaca Chain refers to a group of Wisconsin lakes that exemplify Type IIIa diatom maxima. These lakes maintain permanent climax oxygen maxima throughout summer stratification at temperatures of 15-20°C and light intensities of 5-28% surface intensity, dominated by diatoms such as Fragilaria and Asterionella.

## Weighted Usable Area
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Weighted Usable Area (WUA) is a quantitative metric used in instream flow assessments that combines habitat availability with habitat suitability to determine the total amount of usable space for fish populations at different stream flows. By weighting physical habitat parameters such as depth, velocity, and substrate type according to species-specific preference curves, WUA provides fisheries managers with a biologically meaningful measure of carrying capacity that directly relates river discharge to fish habitat quality and quantity. This approach has been instrumental in establishing environmental flow requirements for fish recovery and conservation in regulated river systems.

### Context
> This technical paper, by David L. Wegner of the [[U.S. Fish And Wildlife Service]] Instream Flow and Aquatic Systems Group in Fort Collins, Colorado, develops an aquatic mitigation plan for the [[Uinta River]] in Utah. The [[Central Utah Project]], including the proposed [[Uinta Dam And...
> -- *[[TheDevelopmentOfAnAquaticMitig_19674]]*

> This U.S. Department of the Interior, National Biological Service primer (Biological Report 29, March 1995) introduces the [[Instream Flow Incremental Methodology]] (IFIM), a interdisciplinary framework for evaluating how water management decisions affect aquatic habitat in flowing waters. The...

## Weir Aeration

### Summary
Weir aeration is the increase in dissolved oxygen that occurs as water falls over a weir and mixes with air. The authors used a deficit-ratio equation to predict weir performance, and their calculations matched observed values reasonably well at Lock Weir and Ducksluice Farm Weir. They found that a high-performing weir could raise DO much more than surface reaeration alone, though it also slows upstream flow and encourages fine sediment to settle.

## Wellton-Mohawk Gila River Flood Channel Restoration Project

### Summary
A proposed $13.7 million flood control project that would channelize 56.2 miles of the Gila River in Arizona between Wellton and Mohawk, moving 5.7 million cubic yards of material and placing 1 million yards of rip-rap along dike interiors. The Southwest Center for Biological Diversity challenged the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers' 'Finding of No Significant Impact' for this project in 1994, arguing it would violate NEPA requirements and permanently foreclose recovery options for Arizona's imperiled native fish fauna by eliminating natural river processes essential for their survival.

## Wellton-Mohawk Irrigation Project

### Summary
The Wellton-Mohawk Irrigation Project in Arizona pumped saline water from a fossil aquifer into the Gila River, which flowed into the Colorado River, causing water salinity delivered to Mexico to increase from 800 parts-per-million to 2,700 ppm by 1960. This created a major diplomatic crisis as Mexican farmers in the Mexicali Valley found their fields coated with white salt residue.

Wendell Minckley was a prominent ichthyologist and desert fish biologist whose expertise on Sonoran Desert fishes and aquatic ecosystems made him a key scientific figure in Colorado River Basin fish conservation efforts during the late 20th century. ## West Fork San Luis Rey River

### Summary
The West Fork San Luis Rey River (WFSLRR) is a second-order headwater stream in San Diego County, California, with a bank-full width generally less than 4 meters that becomes intermittent in summer in high-gradient sections. This source describes it as the location of a successful Black Bullhead removal project where the invasive catfish concentrated in 16 large perennial pools during the 2012-2016 California drought, and where a 1.5-meter upstream dam likely prevented downstream movement of Black Bullhead from a source stock pond. High-flow events in 2017 and 2019 helped flush remaining invasive fish and improved spawning substrate for the native Coastal Rainbow Trout population by removing accumulated fine sediments.

### Summary
West Turkey Creek is a perennial stream in the Rio Yaqui drainage basin of southeastern Arizona that serves as critical habitat for federally endangered Yaqui Chub (Gila purpurea) and other native cyprinids, making it a focal site for interagency conservation efforts coordinated by the Southeast Arizona Native Fish Team. Fish surveys of West Turkey Creek documented significant invasive species pressure, particularly the non-native fathead minnow (Pimephales promelas), which numerically dominated native populations and posed threats to the persistence of endemic species in this isolated aquatic refuge. The creek's ecological conditions and native fish assemblages made it a priority conservation project for regional fisheries management agencies seeking to protect Rio Yaqui basin endemics from extinction during the mid-1990s.

## Western Blacknose Dace
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Western Blacknose Dace (*Rhinichthys atratulus meleagris*) is a small, short-lived cyprinid fish native to eastern North American stream systems, notable for its ecological role as a common inhabitant of cool, spring-fed tributaries and its utility as a model organism for studying age, growth, and reproductive dynamics in temperate freshwater ecosystems. This species has been the subject of detailed ichthyological research examining its population demography, life history characteristics, and reproductive biology in order to better understand the population dynamics and ecological requirements of small stream-dwelling cyprinids. The Western Blacknose Dace serves as an important indicator species for assessing the health and integrity of small stream ecosystems in the Ohio River drainage and adjacent regions.

## Western Energy Expansion Study

### Summary
The Western Energy Expansion Study was a 1977 proposal to expand Glen Canyon Dam's power generation capacity by adding 250 megawatts of new power plant capacity and uprating existing turbines by 104 megawatts. This study was part of the Bureau of Reclamation's shift toward maximizing hydroelectric revenue generation, which contributed to more extreme peaking power operations and accelerated environmental degradation in the Colorado River ecosystem downstream of the dam.

## Western Mosquitofish
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Western Mosquitofish (Gambusia affinis) is a small, highly adaptable livebearing fish native to the southeastern United States that has become one of the most widespread and problematic invasive species in southwestern aquatic ecosystems, particularly in Arizona's desert streams and springs. In conservation biology and aquatic ecology contexts, this species is documented as a significant threat to native fishes, especially federally endangered species like the Gila Topminnow, through predation on larvae and juveniles, aggressive competition for resources, and ecological displacement. Its presence in isolated desert water systems represents a major management challenge for fisheries scientists and conservation biologists working to protect endemic species in fragmented southwestern watersheds.

### Summary
The Western Spotfin Shiner (Notropis spilopterus hypsisomatus) is a subspecies of the spotfin shiner that Gibbs described as new in this 1957 paper, based on a holotype from Wonder Lake Bay, Illinois. It is found in the Illinois and Spring Rivers, other upper Mississippi and Ozark tributaries, and the Gasconade, and the author considers it deeper-bodied with a higher modal body depth than the eastern form. ## Western Yellow-billed Cuckoo

### Summary
The Western Yellow-billed Cuckoo is one of six bird species targeted for conservation in the Lower Colorado River Multi-Species Conservation Program. This species is associated with riparian habitats along the Lower Colorado River, which historically supported large populations but has been severely reduced due to loss of cottonwood-willow habitat over the past century. The conservation strategy proposes adding 12,000-16,000 acres of riparian vegetation to support this and other riparian bird species, though the peer review panel noted this represents a relatively small quantity compared to the hundreds of thousands of acres removed, suggesting recovery is likely to be modest.

## Westfield Creek

### Summary
Westfield Creek was a 3.5-mile Wisconsin stream with flow of 17.2 cfs, pH 8.8, and temperature 77°F where antimycin A non-repellency was demonstrated. Fish showed no downstream escape attempts when exposed to 10 ppb maintained for 10 hours, with mortality occurring in sequence from darters (2 hours) to carp (8 hours), and post-treatment electrofishing finding only small minnows and lampreys surviving.

## Westwater Canyon
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Westwater Canyon is a geomorphologically significant reach of the Upper Colorado River system, located between the Piceance Creek area and Cataract Canyon, that serves as critical habitat for native fish species including the Colorado Pikeminnow and other sympatric fishes in regional monitoring and conservation studies. As a defined study segment within the larger Colorado River basin, Westwater Canyon has been a focal point for interagency standardized monitoring protocols and investigations into how flow regimes, physical habitat structure, and canyon morphology influence native fish population dynamics and early life stage survival. This canyon reach represents an important reference area for understanding river ecosystem responses and evaluating the effectiveness of water management and conservation strategies targeting endangered and threatened fish species across the upper Colorado River system.

## Wheeler Reservoir
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Wheeler Reservoir is a mainstream impoundment on the Tennessee River in northern Alabama created by the Tennessee Valley Authority that fundamentally altered the region's aquatic ecosystem and fisheries by converting a free-flowing riverine system into a stable, stratified reservoir environment. Since its construction in the early 20th century, the reservoir has served as a critical study site for ichthyological research investigating how dam impoundment affects fish community composition, species abundance, habitat utilization patterns, and both commercial and recreational fishery dynamics. The reservoir's relatively stable water levels and distinct habitat zones have made it particularly valuable for long-term fisheries management studies, including pioneering research on largemouth bass population dynamics and exploitation rates through tag-and-recapture methods.

## Whirling Disease
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Whirling disease is a parasitic infection caused by the myxozoan parasite *Myxobolus cerebralis* that affects salmonid fishes, particularly young trout and salmon, causing neurological damage that leads to characteristic spinning or whirling behavior and skeletal deformities. The disease has significant implications for wild fish populations and hatchery operations, as it can cause high mortality rates in affected populations and spread through water systems, making it a major concern in fisheries management and conservation efforts. Whirling disease is often discussed in the context of hatchery programs and fish stocking practices, as artificial propagation and translocation of fish can facilitate the spread of the parasite to naive wild populations.

### Summary
Whiskey Creek is a headwater stream in the West Fork Gila River drainage that supports one of only four pure Gila Trout populations confirmed by this genetic study. ### Summary
The White Amur, commonly known as grass carp (*Ctenopharyngodon idella*), is a large Asian cyprinid fish introduced to aquatic systems as a biological control agent for managing aquatic vegetation. In the context of aquatic ecology and conservation, the White Amur represents both a management tool and a potential ecological concern, as its effectiveness in controlling nuisance plant growth must be weighed against potential impacts on native fish communities and aquatic ecosystems. Its use in regulated fisheries and aquatic management systems reflects the tension between intentional species introductions for specific management objectives and broader conservation goals protecting native aquatic biodiversity.

White Bass (*Morone chrysops*) is a freshwater fish species of significant importance in North American fisheries and aquatic ecology, particularly in large river systems and reservoirs where it serves as both a valued sport fish and an ecological indicator species. The species is characterized by its migratory behavior and variable growth rates depending on habitat conditions, as documented through age and growth studies in systems like the Mississippi River and reservoir environments. ## White Catfish
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
White catfish (Ameiurus catus) are a North American freshwater species belonging to the family Ictaluridae that have become established in various aquatic ecosystems, particularly in coastal and inland waterways where they are studied for their growth rates, population dynamics, and ecological interactions. As both a subject of aquaculture research and an invasive species in some regions, white catfish are important to fisheries management and conservation biology due to their ecological impacts on native fish communities and their role as hosts for parasites and pathogens in aquatic environments. Their ability to adapt to diverse habitats and reproduce successfully makes them significant for understanding invasion dynamics and competition in modified freshwater ecosystems.

## White Crappie
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
White Crappie (*Pomoxis annularis*) is a freshwater sunfish species in the family Centrarchidae that serves as an important food and game fish in North American inland waters. This species is commonly stocked in lakes and reservoirs for recreational fishing and is often managed alongside other panfish species like Black Crappie and Bluegill as part of integrated fisheries management programs. White Crappie populations are valued both for their ecological role in controlling smaller fish populations and their significance to sport and subsistence fishing communities.

## White Grub

### Summary
White Grub is a trematode parasite that encysts specifically in the livers of bass family fish. In heavily infected fish, the grubs can become so numerous that they outweigh the liver tissue itself, representing a significant pathological burden on the host.

The Tribe engages in fisheries science initiatives including fish hatchery operations, rearing programs, and participation in regional management discussions related to aquatic resource stewardship. ## White Mountains
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The White Mountains refers to a mountain range in east-central Arizona that serves as a critical refuge for endemic and native aquatic species, most notably the Apache Trout, and encompasses isolated headwater creek systems that represent discrete populations valuable for conservation and fisheries management. This high-elevation region functions as a "sky island" ecosystem characterized by isolated aquatic habitats that support unique freshwater fauna, including both fish and mollusk communities adapted to coldwater conditions. The geographic and hydrologic isolation of White Mountains creek systems has made the region a focal point for native fish conservation efforts and population restoration projects in the American Southwest.

## White Oak Embayment

### Summary
White Oak Embayment is a location in the Clinch River system where silt was found to trap radioactivity effectively. Centrifuged silt from this embayment showed 2 × 10⁴ counts/min/gram while the supernatant water was near background levels (August 31, 1956), demonstrating how suspended particles in the Clinch River water capture and transport radioactive materials.

## White Pelican

### Summary
White Pelican showed much greater susceptibility to toxaphene than DDT in feeding studies. A diet containing 50 ppm toxaphene caused tremors, convulsions, and death in 4-6 weeks, while 50 ppm DDT diet eliminated parasites but caused no mortality.

## White Perch
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
White Perch (*Morone americana*) is a panfish species that occupies an important ecological and management niche in North American warm-water fisheries, serving as both a prey species for apex predators and a direct harvest target for recreational anglers. In the context of mid-20th century fisheries management, white perch populations were particularly vulnerable to stunting and recruitment overshoot when predator populations were inadequate, requiring active management interventions such as selective removal to maintain sustainable populations with sufficient numbers of harvestable-sized individuals above legal minimum lengths.

## White Pine County

### Summary
White Pine County, Nevada is the location of Shoshone Ponds, one of three current refugia for the Pahrump Killifish. The site, located approximately 50 km southeast of Ely on Bureau of Land Management land, has maintained reproducing populations in 3 of 5 ponds since transplantation efforts began in 1972.

## White River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The White River refers to a river system in the south-central United States (primarily Arkansas) that has been extensively studied in ichthyological research due to dramatic ecological transformations resulting from dam construction and reservoir development. Originally a warmwater riverine system, the upper White River was fundamentally altered by the sequential construction of four multipurpose dams (Beaver Dam, Table Rock Lake, Lake Taneycomb, and Bull Shoals Reservoir), which converted it into a series of cold-water impoundments and affected both fish communities and hatchery operations. The river system has been a focus of fish conservation and distribution studies, including work on species recovery programs and the monitoring of native fish populations in the context of habitat modification.

## White River (Arizona)
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The White River in Arizona is a significant coldwater aquatic system in the state's east-central region that serves as critical habitat for native and introduced trout populations, particularly in reaches below the confluence of the Snake and Ord Creeks. As documented in mid-20th century fisheries management assessments, the White River was central to proposed water resource development projects and their evaluation for impacts on fish conservation and recreational fisheries. The river's ecological importance made it a focal point for interagency review processes examining how dam construction and water management decisions would affect trout stream productivity and aquatic biodiversity.

## White River (Nevada)
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The White River in Nevada is a significant desert stream system within the southwestern United States that serves as critical habitat for endemic and threatened fish species, particularly the Desert Sucker (*Catostomus clarki*) and other native fishes adapted to arid aquatic environments. This river drainage represents an important component of regional fish biodiversity surveys and conservation documentation efforts, as evidenced by its inclusion in comprehensive ichthyological inventories of southwestern fish distributions and taxonomic assessments. Understanding the White River's fish fauna and ecological conditions is essential for conservation planning in desert aquatic ecosystems where water scarcity and anthropogenic impacts threaten the persistence of native fish populations.

### Summary
White River Arkansas is a location where large, old buffalo fish specimens were documented. Fish aged 13-15 years from this river weighed over 40 pounds, providing evidence for the species' growth potential under favorable natural conditions.

## White River Dam

### Summary
White River Dam was a proposed water development project that posed a threat to endangered Colorado River fishes through water depletion and habitat modification. By 1987, the project had been indefinitely postponed, which led the Recovery Team to recommend reopening Section 7 consultation for the Taylor Draw Dam/Kenney Reservoir project due to this new information about the White River's importance.

## White Sturgeon
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
White Sturgeon (Acipenser transmontanus) is a large, long-lived anadromous and potamodromous fish species belonging to the family Acipenseridae, characterized by a cartilaginous skeleton with bony plates and found in freshwater systems of western North America, particularly the Columbia River drainage. As a keystone species in riverine ecosystems, White Sturgeon plays an important ecological role and has significant conservation value, though populations have declined substantially due to dam construction, habitat fragmentation, and overfishing, prompting restoration efforts including hatchery programs like those operated at the Abernathy Salmon Culture Technology Center. The species is notable for its inclusion in comprehensive ichthyological surveys and represents one of North America's native sturgeon species requiring multi-jurisdictional management and conservation strategies to maintain viable wild populations.

## White Sucker
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The White Sucker (Catostomus commersoni) is a widespread North American cypriniform fish in the family Catostomidae, commonly found in streams and rivers across diverse freshwater habitats where it occupies pools and slower-current zones. In ichthyological surveys and taxonomic studies, White Suckers serve as reference specimens for comparative anatomy, particularly in larval identification where diagnostic characters such as interneural bone morphology and fontanelle structure are used to distinguish them from co-occurring sucker species. As a bottom-feeding generalist with relatively high environmental tolerance, the White Sucker is an important indicator species in stream ecology assessments and fish community analysis, often representing a significant component of regional fish faunas in North American aquatic systems.

### Summary

Whiteriver is a town on the White Mountain Apache Reservation in Arizona that is the intended recipient of water from the proposed diversion project. The project aims to supply 480,000,000 gallons per year to meet the town's projected water demand through 2022, with municipal and industrial demand expected to increase from 319,000,000 gallons per year at Phase I start to 480,000,000 gallons per year during Phase II.

## Whiteriver gauge

### Summary
Whiteriver gauge is a stream flow measurement station on the White River used to assess cumulative water withdrawal impacts on Loach Minnow habitat. Total existing diversions within the Loach Minnow range (28.4 cubic feet per second) represent 45% of the mean median July discharge measured at this gauge.

## Whitewater Canyons

### Summary
Whitewater canyons are turbulent river reaches characterized by deep pools, eddies, and spring inputs that maintain perennial flow. In the Colorado River system, these canyon reaches provided critical refugia for native fishes during drought periods, with adult humpback chubs specifically occupying deep swirling eddies in turbulent canyon reaches near boulders.

## Whitewater Draw
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Whitewater Draw is a tributary drainage system in the Rio Yaqui basin of southeastern Arizona that has served as a focus for ichthyological surveys assessing native fish fauna composition and distribution within the southwestern United States. Beyond its contemporary ichthyological interest, the system is significant to fisheries and conservation science for its paleoenvironmental and paleohydrological records, which document shifts between braided stream conditions during wetter Pleistocene periods and more arid Holocene conditions that would have substantially altered aquatic habitat availability and fish community structure. Understanding the Late Quaternary alluvial history and climate changes recorded in Whitewater Draw's stratigraphy provides essential context for interpreting present-day fish assemblages and informing conservation strategies for native species in this arid-region drainage.

## Whitewater Lake, Nebraska

### Summary
Whitewater Lake, Nebraska was a 490-acre lake with highly alkaline water (pH 9.3) and temperature of 62°F where antimycin A was tested. At this high pH, 7.3 ppb degraded before achieving complete fish kill, and bioassays indicated 12-15 ppb would be needed, demonstrating that antimycin degrades rapidly at pH above 8.5.

## Whitewood Creek

### Summary
Whitewood Creek in South Dakota was heavily polluted with rock powder from mining operations, reaching 22% settleable solids by volume and turbidity above 720 APHA units. This pollution completely eliminated all fish species from the creek, with fish populations only reappearing when pollution was reduced to 3% by volume.

## Whitmore Lake

### Summary
Whitmore Lake is a 675-acre warm-water lake located in Livingston and Washtenaw counties, Michigan, connected to the Huron River drainage. ## Whitneyville Dam

### Summary
A dam on the Machias River that the report identifies as the most serious obstacle to salmon recovery. ## Wild Trout
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Wild trout are self-sustaining populations of trout that reproduce naturally in their native stream habitats, existing within the ecological carrying capacity of those systems. In contrast to hatchery-reared trout used for supplemental stocking, wild trout populations serve as important indicators of ecosystem health and are central to conservation biology efforts that prioritize habitat restoration and ecosystem management approaches. The conservation and management of wild trout populations requires understanding their relationship to stream habitat quality, as natural recruitment and population sustainability depend directly on adequate physical and chemical conditions rather than artificial propagation.

### Summary

## William J. Matthews
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
William J. Matthews is a pioneering ichthyologist and aquatic ecologist whose influential work in the 1980s established foundational frameworks for understanding fish community structure and organization in North American stream systems. His research systematically examined how fish assemblages vary along stream gradients and whether classification systems like stream order effectively predict community composition, thereby contributing essential theoretical and methodological approaches to modern fish ecology and conservation biology. Matthews's studies laid crucial groundwork for subsequent investigations into longitudinal patterns in fish communities and the ecological factors structuring fish assemblages in flowing waters.

Willow Beach National Fish Hatchery is a U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service facility located on the Lower Colorado River that serves as a critical captive breeding and rearing center for endangered Colorado River native fish species, particularly bonytail chub (*Gila elegans*) and razorback suckers (*Xyrauchen texanus*). The hatchery receives wild-origin broodstock collected from Lake Mohave and other Colorado River reservoirs to maintain genetically diverse captive populations and produce juvenile fish for reintroduction into the wild, making it essential to recovery efforts for these critically endangered species mandated under biological opinions. This facility represents a cornerstone of the Colorado River's native fish recovery program, combining ex situ conservation breeding with strategic stocking to supplement declining wild populations.

## Willow Beach NFH

### Summary
Willow Beach National Fish Hatchery is a federal fish production facility in Arizona participating in endangered species recovery efforts. In the 1987-1988 stocking plan, this hatchery was allocated 200,000 Colorado squawfish fry as part of the intermediate rearing program designed to grow endangered fish to larger, less vulnerable sizes before their release into historic river habitats.

### Context
> This March 1987 correspondence from the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service Regional Director to the Arizona Game and Fish Department outlines a comprehensive reintroduction program for threatened and endangered native fishes in Arizona waters. ## Willow Beach Resort

### Summary
Willow Beach is a location approximately 12 miles downstream from Hoover Dam in Black Canyon where daily water level fluctuations were measured at 0.5 feet under existing conditions and would increase to 2 feet under the proposed powerplant modification. The last bonytail chubs collected in Black Canyon were taken at Willow Beach 20 years prior to this 1981 assessment, and the area above Willow Beach has extremely limited eddy and pool habitat preferred by bonytails.

### Context

## Willow Creek Reservoir

### Summary
Willow Creek Reservoir was gill-netted as part of the rough fish control program in 1953. The netting operation captured 5 suckers and 3 trout from this water body.

## Windermere
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Windermere is a large, oligotrophic (nutrient-poor) lake in the English Lake District that has served as a major research site for aquatic ecology and fisheries science studies, particularly regarding fish physiology, limnological processes, and the ecology of economically and ecologically important species such as perch (*Perca fluviatilis*) and pike. The lake's well-characterized physical and chemical characteristics, including its contrasting profundal sediment dynamics compared to more eutrophic systems, have made it an ideal reference ecosystem for investigating seasonal variations in fish reproductive physiology, biogeochemical cycling in lake sediments, and relict fauna. Windermere's role as a long-term study site has provided foundational data for understanding how fish populations and aquatic biogeochemistry respond to environmental gradients and temporal variation in natural freshwater systems.

## Winkler Method
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Winkler Method is a classical chemical titration technique used to measure dissolved oxygen concentrations in aquatic environments, which is critical for assessing fish habitat quality and water body productivity in fisheries research. This method involves fixing oxygen in water samples through chemical reactions and subsequently titrating the resulting compound to quantify the exact dissolved oxygen content, making it a precise and widely-used standard for monitoring aquatic conditions in reservoirs, coves, and other fish habitats. The technique was particularly valuable in mid-20th century fisheries studies for establishing baseline oxygen levels that directly influence fish distribution, survival, and the carrying capacity of aquatic ecosystems.

## Winnipeg

### Summary
Winnipeg is a Canadian city that represented the primary market for Montana Goldeye, where the species commanded premium prices ($1.50-$1.95/pound retail) as a smoked delicacy served in restaurants and railroad dining cars. However, by September 1967, 400,000 pounds sat in cold storage there, causing prices to fishermen to collapse from 45-50¢/pound to as low as 17¢/pound, and Montana fishermen reported being cheated on weight and price by wholesalers in this market.

## Winterkill
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Winterkill refers to the die-off of fish populations that occurs during winter, typically in shallow or ice-covered lakes and ponds where oxygen depletion becomes critical due to reduced light penetration and photosynthetic activity under ice. This phenomenon is particularly significant in fisheries management and conservation biology, as winterkill can dramatically reduce or eliminate fish populations in affected water bodies, making it an important consideration when evaluating stocking programs, assessing fish habitat quality, and managing put-and-take fisheries. Understanding winterkill dynamics is essential for predicting survival rates of stocked fingerlings and natural populations in temperate aquatic ecosystems.

## Winters Doctrine

### Summary
The Winters Doctrine is the federal reserved water rights principle that stems from Winters v. United States (1908). The source notes it was extended in 1971 to all federal reservations. It identifies three methods for quantifying reserved water: the irrigable acres test, the open-ended method, and the present needs and uses application. A pending federal case on behalf of the Papago Indian Tribe applies this doctrine to pumping under the San Xavier Reservation.

## Wood Buffalo National Park

### Summary
Wood Buffalo National Park in Canada, near the Arctic Circle, is the breeding grounds for wild Whooping Cranes, discovered in 1954 following a fire. The park's short ice-free period represents a major hazard to breeding success, as young cranes must reach flight capability in approximately 90 days before heavy fall freezes, and failed first nesting attempts cannot be successfully repeated due to the abbreviated summer season. The vast, remote nature of the park makes complete population counts impossible.

## World Conservation Monitoring Centre

### Summary
The World Conservation Monitoring Centre (WCMC) is a key partner organization that manages global biodiversity information and collaborates with SSC. WCMC cooperates in producing IUCN Red Lists and provides data services, database development assistance, and geographic information systems support to Specialist Groups. The Centre plays a crucial role in SSC's information management infrastructure, supporting the Commission's work in species data standardization and distribution.

## World Conservation Strategy

### Summary
The World Conservation Strategy (1980) is a foundational document that provides part of the guiding philosophy for SSC's work. It established three key principles that guide the Commission: conservation of biological diversity, preservation of life-support systems, and sustainable use of living natural resources. This strategy was succeeded by the document 'Caring for the Earth' (1991), whose principles SSC aims to implement.

## Woundfin
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Woundfin (Plagopterus argentissimus) is a small, endemic cyprinid fish native to the Virgin River drainage in the southwestern United States that has become a species of significant conservation concern due to severe population declines and habitat loss. The species is threatened by water diversions, habitat degradation, competition from introduced fishes, and predation, making it the subject of ongoing recovery efforts including habitat restoration and management initiatives. As a endemic desert stream fish with specialized ecological requirements, the woundfin represents a critical indicator of Virgin River ecosystem health and has been central to debates over water management and native fish conservation in the American Southwest.

## Woundfin Recovery Team
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Woundfin Recovery Team is a collaborative conservation effort established to address the endangerment and potential extinction of the woundfin (*Plagopterus argentissimus*), a small native fish species found in the Virgin River drainage of the southwestern United States. This interdisciplinary team coordinates research, habitat restoration, and management strategies aimed at stabilizing wild populations and developing recovery protocols for this threatened species. The team's work exemplifies the organized, multi-agency approach required for endangered fish conservation in arid western aquatic ecosystems facing threats from habitat degradation, water management practices, and non-native species competition.

## Wray Hatchery

### Summary

## Wretlind

## Wyland Lake

### Summary
Wyland Lake is an example of a Type I pseudo-metalimnetic oxygen maximum lake, where a temporary oxygen maximum of 19.3 mg/L developed at 2 meters depth on July 11, 1955. This type of maximum forms during June-July calm periods under secondary thermoclines in small, protected lakes but disappears when wind-induced circulation incorporates the layer into the epilimnion.

## Wyoming Toad

### Summary
The Wyoming Toad is an endangered amphibian threatened by all three pesticides (carbaryl, malathion, and acephate) which can affect both aquatic larvae and terrestrial adults that feed on arthropods. The reasonable and prudent alternative requires a 1/4-mile buffer on each side of the Little Laramie River in Albany County, Wyoming, to protect this species from jeopardy.

### Context

## Wyoming Water Association

### Summary
Wyoming Water Association is one of the partner organizations in the Upper Colorado River Endangered Fish Recovery Program established in 1988. The program brings together water users, government agencies, conservation groups, and other stakeholders to recover endangered fish species in the Colorado River Basin.

## Wyoming Water Development Association

### Summary
The Wyoming Water Development Association is one of the participating organizations in the Upper Colorado River Recovery Program. This association represents Wyoming water development interests in the cooperative agreement established in 1988 to balance endangered species recovery with continued water development in the Upper Colorado River Basin.

## Wyoming Water Resources Research Institute

### Summary
A state-based research institute that partly funded this study through the Office of Water Research and Technology under the Water Resources Research Act of 1964. The report is issued as Water Resources Series No. 50.

## Wyoming Wildlife Federation

### Summary
The Wyoming Wildlife Federation is one of the participating organizations in the Upper Colorado River Recovery Program. This wildlife conservation group was part of the 1988 cooperative agreement that established the collaborative framework for recovering endangered fish species while accommodating water development needs.
